## AI Research

### [Mnemonic portraits for 19,023 human genes](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/BJ7AqXeigNKXLqZyx/mnemonic-portraits-for-19-023-human-genes)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Back in 2013, Scott Alexander wrote in </span><a href="https://slatestarcodex.com/2013/08/14/extreme-mnemonics/"><span>Extreme mnemonics</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><i><span>JS-154 is one of five metabolic products of netamine; however, the enzyme that produces it is unknown. It is manufactured in cells in the far rostral region of of the cerebrum, but after binding with a leukocynoid it takes a role in maintaining the blood-brain barrier – in particular guiding the movements of lipid molecules.</span></i></p><p><span>I find I can read paragraphs like this five or six times, write them on flashcards, enter them into Anki, and my brain still refuses to understand or remember them after weeks of trying.</span></p><p><span>On the other hand, </span><b><span>my brain easily remembers vastly more complicated structures when they’re loaded with human-accessible meaning.</span></b><span> For example, just by casually reading the Game of Thrones series, I know an extremely intricate web of genealogies, alliances, locations, journeys, battlesites, et cetera. Byte for byte, an average Game of Thrones reader/viewer probably has as much Game of Thrones information as a neuroscience Ph.D has molecular biology information, but getting the neuroscience info is still a thousand times harder.</span><br /><span>[…]</span><br /><span>This makes me wonder if it would be possible to produce </span><b><span>a story as enjoyable as Game of Thrones which was actually isomorphic to the most important pathways in molecular biology</span></b><span>.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>It's 2026 and we now have LLMs and image generation models. Is the mnemonic worldbuilding project of this scale now remotely feasible?</span></p><p><span>Here's my attempt at the first piece of it: the characters.</span></p><h1><span>What molecules should we map to the characters?</span></h1><p><span>There already exist works of fiction that map human cell types to memorable characters. </span></p><p><i><span>Osmosis Jones</span></i><span> asks: what if each cell was a cartoon character?</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 49.69788519637463%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779919181/lexical_client_uploads/sp3h0gdymfq61ckyphor.png" /></figure><p><i><span>Cells at Work</span></i><span> asks: what if each cell was an anime character</span><i><span>?</span></i></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 49.61480546934007%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779919212/lexical_client_uploads/gmwhdzujqbfxyatpd25k.png" /></figure><p><i><span>Cells at Work Code Black</span></i><span> asks: what if each cell was desperately fighting for survival in the body of an aging impotent smoker?</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 49.7129918585371%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779919380/lexical_client_uploads/urnnzfinrj3847uqo8pk.png" /></figure><p><span>I found these worlds delightful and I do recommend them for students just getting into physiology. </span></p><p><span>However, the deeper I got into molecular biology, the more I started to find this "1 cell = 1 character" mapping mnemonically futile.</span></p><p><span>From single-cell sequencing experiments, we know that cell types are not rigid essentialist bins, but are more like attractors in the analog gene expression space. Individual cells routinely change their type-cluster membership during regular development and regeneration. A given cell could have one "type" today and another one tomorrow. You can't really ask </span><i><span>How many cell types are there? - </span></i><span>different cell databases categorize human cells into anywhere from </span><a href="https://www.proteinatlas.org/humanproteome/single%2Bcell/single%2Bcell%2Btype" rel="noreferrer"><span>154</span></a><span> to </span><a href="https://academic.oup.com/nar/article/51/D1/D870/6775381" rel="noreferrer"><span>1715 </span></a><span>cell types.</span></p><p><span>But you can ask </span><i><span>How many protein-coding genes are there?</span></i><span> totally fine. The answer, in humans, is around 19 thousand. Gene boundaries are a lot more digital and measurement-independent than cell type boundaries. So the natural mnemonic mapping is the one where cells are more like vehicles, cities, or pocket universes - inhabited by gene characters. </span></p><p><span>19 thousand is a lot of characters to memorize. But it will be roughly the same number of characters today, in 10 years, or in 1,000 years, all keeping the same names</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefq1qxbbo4xu"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnq1qxbbo4xu">[1]</a></sup></span><span>. So it's worth starting to get familiar with them today.</span></p><h1><span>Isomorphisms</span></h1><p><span>To generate the visual descriptions of the characters, I needed to download gene data, and to come up with memorable isomorphisms.</span></p><p><span>Getting data for 19k genes was easy - I already had most of it from my previous project, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/kLdADoEtdYf6FDM8j/i-made-geneguessr" rel="noreferrer"><span>Geneguessr</span></a><span>. Bioinformatics datasets have useful per-gene metrics to work with, like protein mass, mutation tolerance, a one-paragraph verbal description, and clan membership.</span></p><p><span>Getting isomorphisms right was extremely difficult, and LLM suggestions didn't help much. After a few months of brainstorming and reshuffling, here is what I settled on:</span></p><p><b><span>Character sex </span></b><span>→</span><b><span> protein transmembrane status.</span></b></p><p><span>Male = transmembrane protein. Female = soluble protein.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="LAIR.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780003491/lexical_client_uploads/m0pem3voynbz1wjbtony.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>LAIR1, male; LAIR2, female</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Sex needs to be mapped to something that splits proteins into two roughly equal-sized categorical bins. I found transmembrane status very important to know when studying cell signalling pathways, so I'm happy to keep it prominent, even if the sex ratio is somewhat skewed.</span></p><p><span>73% of genes became female, 27% male.</span></p><p><b><span>Character weight </span></b><span>→</span><b><span> protein mass.</span></b></p><p><span>45 kg = 45 kilo</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dalton_(unit)"><span>dalton</span></a><span> (kDa).</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="mass.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780003494/lexical_client_uploads/hybi8jcql903zxckguwz.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>IGHJ1, 2 kg; TTN, 3816 kg</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>I first experimented with mapping height to amino acid count, but that mapping covered too much dynamic range outside of usual human variation. Amino acid count and protein mass are in a linear relationship, but human weight scales with height squared.</span></p><p><span>For each gene, I picked the "mass" to match the mass of the top protein isoform when searching that gene in the Uniprot database. This is raw sequence-derived mass, which doesn't account for post-translational maturation steps. There can also be many alternative protein isoforms per gene, which I associate with multiple isoforms of the same character (think regular Goku vs Super Saiyan Goku).</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780003489/lexical_client_uploads/d7yjflqzdwv7soybrdpy.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Weight distribution histogram across genome</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><b><span>Character age </span></b><span>→</span><b><span> year of discovery</span></b><span>, with 2020 as the zero point.</span></p><p><span>Gene named in the year 2000 becomes a 20 y.o. character.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="age.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780003491/lexical_client_uploads/oyylnavja5ewolrfi4ey.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>MYMX, 3 y.o.; KEL, 63 y.o.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>My first instinct was to map character age to gene evolutionary age. However, there's a lot of uncertainty with both data and measurement models of gene evolution. Definitional nitpicks can easily swing the gene age from being ancient to being very young. Plus that would make most characters into deep elders.</span></p><p><span>Mapping age to discovery year has bonus mnemonic benefits: the oldest-looking characters become the most "important" in terms of prominence. There are also very few characters who look under-18 but have a huge mass, sparing us from the "huge baby" problem somewhat.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780003489/lexical_client_uploads/n9gfamvi5jrjog8umbjj.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Age distribution histogram across the genome</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><p><b><span>Fashion style </span></b><span>→</span><b><span> Pfam clan.</span></b></p><p><span>The style categorization I really like is</span><a href="https://aesthetics.fandom.com/wiki/Aesthetic_Visual_Index"><span> Aestheticswiki</span></a><span>: a wiki of around 1,000 pages devoted to various strains of historical fashion, subcultures, interior design, and web design. So my goal was to find a protein dataset that sorts most human proteins into 200-700 bins, 1-3 bins per protein, with similar genes getting into the same bin.</span></p><p><span>Pfam clan database sorts human proteins among 563 structural folds ("clans") like "Beta-propeller" and "Cystine-knot". Many genes get 0 Pfam clans, six genes get 7 Pfam clans simultaneously, but overall I'm quite happy with the dimensionality here.</span></p><p><span>What I'm still not quite happy with is the mapping itself. Turns out, mapping protein folds to fashion styles is some kind of a post-singularity problem. All LLM suggestions I got basically boiled down to "make a 500x800 table and score each square". I spent so much time trying out more principled approaches, playing around with matching Qwen3 embeddings of aesthetics to ESM embeddings of clans, up to looking at the Optimal Transport method and such. In the end, nothing beats just asking Claude to look through the pairings and reassign the badly matching ones in a loop.</span></p><p><span>My real stroke of luck was noticing that there's 9 peptidase Pfam clans and also 9 types of Goths on Aestheticswiki. Given what peptidases do to other proteins, this seemed like a no-brainer association. After assigning peptidases to Goths, I used this well-matching cluster as a template for Claude to find adjacent clans (in text embedding space) and pick a good adjacent aesthetic to map it to. It took a few months to really harmonize the picks, and many nights of just leaving Claude to click on dropdown pickers in the GUI, but overall the mapping turned out halfway decent.</span></p><p><span>Some examples of aesthetic mappings:</span></p><p><span>Three clans of glycosyltransferases - GT-A, GT-B, and GT-C - map to three Eastern European styles -</span><a href="https://aesthetics.fandom.com/wiki/Russian_2K17"><span> Russian 2K17</span></a><span>,</span><a href="https://aesthetics.fandom.com/wiki/Slavic_Violence_Tumblr"><span> Slavic Violence Tumblr</span></a><span> and</span><a href="https://aesthetics.fandom.com/wiki/Gopnik"><span> Gopnik</span></a><span>.</span></p><img alt="GT.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780003494/lexical_client_uploads/hejfzdln0wtwjma53nnv.png" /><p><a href="https://aesthetics.fandom.com/wiki/Dark_Academia"><span>Dark Academia</span></a><span> maps to C2H2 zinc finger clan,</span><a href="https://aesthetics.fandom.com/wiki/Theatre_Academia"><span> Theatre Academia</span></a><span> maps to RBP11-like,</span><a href="https://aesthetics.fandom.com/wiki/Art_Academia"><span> Art Academia</span></a><span> maps to SHS2 - protein domains found inside the cell nucleus.</span></p><img alt="academia.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780003501/lexical_client_uploads/udb4yybnhgjvmu67ccd4.png" /><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780003491/lexical_client_uploads/kifsqw8wdd9r8hx0yfgf.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Chart of aesthetics across the genome</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><b><span>Fantastic feature </span></b><span>→</span><b><span> Gene symbol stem.</span></b></p><p><span>There are like a thousand different OR (olfactory receptor) genes: OR1A1, OR1A2, and so on all the way to OR52Z1. Sure, they all share a Dark Fantasy aesthetic mapped to the GPCR class A clan, but wouldn't it be nice to reserve memorable features specifically for shared-stem genes like OR? After all, we have to assign cool fantastic features somehow.</span></p><p><span>Some example features I picked:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="IL.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780003492/lexical_client_uploads/rnntsdefpkoityrrlmho.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Demon horns for IL genes (interleukins - inflammation regulators)</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="ZNF.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780003495/lexical_client_uploads/hgnokdyhaunttocj8xms.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Metal hands for ZNF (Zinc finger) genes</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="FOX.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780003491/lexical_client_uploads/zptgrjdkyzzcuw3qchfh.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Fox ears and tail for FOX genes</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="OR.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780003499/lexical_client_uploads/ix72vsohtnb7fagquf9i.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>And for OR genes, pig nose. Sorry.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><p><b><span>Character color </span></b><span>→ uhh</span></p><p><span>This one I struggled with the most.</span></p><p><span>The dimensionality of color is very weird. The perceptual colorspace is shaped like a</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicone"><span> bicone</span></a><span>. More precisely, it can be somewhat approximated with a bicone.</span></p><p><span>What colorspace is </span><i><span>actually </span></i><span>shaped like is something twisted and unholy.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780003490/lexical_client_uploads/w50whd0lsr3u0h9qptbk.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Avoid looking at colorspace for too long</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>My search for bicone-distributed molecular biology metrics came up empty. So I had to come up with individual gene metrics to map to color coordinates. I picked hue, saturation, and lightness as the most intuitive color components.</span></p><p><span>I mapped character </span><b><span>lightness</span></b><span> to </span><b><span>gnomAD LOEUF</span></b><span>.</span></p><p><span>This metric basically tells you how well tolerated mutations in this gene are, from 0.0 (intolerable, black) to 2.0 (tolerable, white).</span></p><p><span>In other words, a low LOEUF score indicates that evolution strongly selects against mutations in this gene: genes highly important for survival will be darker, redundant or miscellaneous genes will be lighter.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780003489/lexical_client_uploads/trv1jztpkbt8c2r8cmtj.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Lightness distribution across the genome</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>I mapped character color </span><b><span>saturation</span></b><span> to </span><b><span>HPA Tau</span></b><span> score.</span></p><p><span>Tau is a measure of gene expression specificity, ranging from 0 (ubiquitous, gray) to 1 (tissue-specific, saturated).</span></p><p><span>So a housekeeping gene expressed in the majority of cell types will be gray, and a cell-specific protein will have a vibrant color.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780003489/lexical_client_uploads/x3wzngf4i3jwzwcx19ei.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Saturation distribution across the genome</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Color hue is different because it's an angular dimension, not a linear one. So the metric needs to be one that doesn't really have "low extreme" or "high extreme", or where the two extremes aren't that functionally different.</span></p><p><span>I chose to simply map the </span><b><span>hue</span></b><span> to the </span><b><span>first letter</span></b><span> of the gene symbol, mostly for mnemonic reasons (to ease the name recall given that you remember the color).</span></p><p><span>As a side benefit, genes that share the same name, like GENE1, GENE2, GENE3, keep somewhat similar colors, varying only in saturation and lightness but not hue.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780003488/lexical_client_uploads/m2cba0edh1ocnyjhp2ze.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Hue distribution across the genome</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Keep in mind that in a bicone, getting too far up or down along the lightness axis restricts your variation along the saturation axis - so you won't get colorful blacks or whites, they'll just look black and white regardless of what hue and saturation you set.</span></p><p><span>So when you meet a character who's void black or laundry white in color, you will know their status of importance, but not their tissue specificity or first letter of their name. I think it's a reasonable trade-off.</span></p><p><span>To sum up, looking at a gene symbol pill in your browser, you will be able to deduce:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>black pill? important gene.</span></li><li value="2"><span>white pill? mutation-tolerant gene.</span></li><li value="3"><span>vibrantly colored pill? tissue-specific gene.</span></li><li value="4"><span>gray pill? ubiquitous gene, medium importance.</span></li></ul><p><span>And if you remember the character's appearance but not their name, recalling their hue gives you a hint to their name.</span></p><h1><span>Generating images</span></h1><p><span>All the above character details, along with the gene function snippet, can be fed to the LLM to make an image prompt ("sample") for the gene.</span></p><p><span>Here's an example of what one sample looks like, for COASY, generated with Claude Opus 4.6:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>coasy. the jacket is what you see first: dove grey wool crepe with rounded shoulders and a nipped waist that cinches with the precision of a corsetiere who studied the masters, the buttons covered in matching fabric, the hem ending exactly at the hip where a full circle skirt picks up the silhouette and billows outward to mid-calf in stiff champagne silk taffeta. the skirt uses enough fabric to tent a field hospital. beneath the jacket, a cream silk blouse with mandarin collar buttoned to the throat, the collar’s shape borrowed from qipao construction — high, stiff, framing her jaw like a pedestal. white kid leather gloves reaching past the wrists. cream satin pumps with kitten heels. her hat is a shallow-brimmed cocktail fascinator in dove grey felt, pinned at an angle with a jade butterfly hairpin whose wings are set with tiny seed pearls. she looks like she’s about to take tea with someone she’s already decided to destroy. sixty-two point three kilograms, twenty-six, female. ash brown skin — the muted warmth of fired umber, darker at the knuckles and undersides of her wrists, lighter across the throat where the mandarin collar frames it. built long and curved: shoulders sloped and rounded, breasts set high against the silk blouse, waist small enough to wrap two hands around, hips flaring into the full skirt like a bell, legs long under the taffeta. her face is oval with a delicate jaw, high cheekbones, a small nose, lips full and painted in matte persimmon. her eyes are narrow and dark and very still — always calculating. her hair is blue-black, pulled into a high topknot wrapped with grey silk ribbon, the rest falling in a single thick rope braid down her back to the waist. she carries a jian — a double-edged straight sword, the blade three feet of folded steel polished to a mercury mirror, the hilt wrapped in grey silk with a jade pommel carved into a peony. she draws it from a scabbard concealed beneath the full skirt, the taffeta parting as she reaches through a hidden slit, and the blade comes out singing. every stroke leaves a trail of silk thread in the air behind the blade, fine as spider web, hanging in the space the jian passed through. the silk hardens in seconds — each thread becoming a razor-thin filament that floats at head height and hip height and ankle height, catching light just as it cuts. she fights by filling the battlefield with silk. three minutes into an engagement the air around her is a lattice of cutting threads and she moves through it remembering where every single one hangs while her opponents move through it like paper being fed into a shredder. she is serene throughout, smiling closed-lipped while the garden of silk around her comes into bloom and everything that touches it comes apart.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>These samples can be optionally processed into comma-separated tags for the image generators that don't accept huge blocks of text. Still, I think it preserves more than half of the designer's intent.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 59.06344410876133%;"><img alt="COASY.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780004460/lexical_client_uploads/fnw4jmb6o3qp5dyye4hv.png" /></figure><p><span>The samples have a tendency to mode collapse to a handful of generic props, such as vials and clocks, but overall they come out diverse enough for our use case, while being decently similar between similar genes, and reflecting the gene activity in a way that's not too literal.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 59.21450151057401%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779624574/lexical_client_uploads/t54t8ndlunbry9y7ax7k.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Alright, maybe sometimes a bit too literal</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Now that we have 19k text samples, we need to turn them into images. Which image generation service to use? My constraints were as follows:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Must not bankrupt me when I queue a 19k image job.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Must have thousands of distinct styles and be easy to switch between them.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Must have decent detail fidelity in single-character compositions.</span></li></ol><p><span>Satisfying all three of those at once is not easy.</span></p><p><span>My personal map of image generation tools in 2026 looks like this: </span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Artboard 1sds.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779910412/lexical_client_uploads/kq4byfzsk6mbgto4jv5a.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Red border = paid services, green border = free local models</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><b><span>Nano Banana</span></b><span> and </span><b><span>ChatGPT image2</span></b><span> are at their most impressive when it comes to detail fidelity (number of fingers), complex prompt following, text accuracy, and multi-character images. However, all of their outputs kind of have this "default settings" feel. Once you've seen the tone mapping pattern, you can't unsee it, and all the outputs kind of end up looking tired. You can maybe get around it with one heavily tailored style prompt, but it will still end up blurring together if you reuse it for 19k images.</span></p><p><span>On the other hand, </span><b><span>Midjourney </span></b><span>still looks stunning in 2026 and has a very useful "sref code" system for seeding aesthetic variability. However, not only is it a paid service, it doesn't even offer API access - I would have to reserve my laptop purely for some kind of browser automation. I'll gladly collaborate with MJ if they offer me some kind of direct access, but for the first pass it will have to wait.</span></p><p><span>In the "free local imagegen" land, </span><b><span>SDXL</span></b><span> and </span><b><span>Z-image</span></b><span> are the two popular local models. I tried them. They're okay. Their LORA ecosystem does offer decent customization if you're willing to download each one manually, but I did find their extensibility too clunky for my taste.</span></p><p><span>What stuck with me was </span><a href="https://huggingface.co/circlestone-labs/Anima" rel="noreferrer"><b><span>Anima</span></b></a><span>. My goodness, how variable it is, and how much it changes the linework and composition just based on which artist names you add to the prompt. Beautiful. Does it generate an extra finger here and there? Perhaps. Does it mess up character color or prop shape? Yes, it happens. But it's a fair price for just how much effortless variability you get on a style level with a single pipeline. All of the gene images you see in this post were produced by a local anima-preview on my laptop without any style prompt changes except for the artist names.</span></p><h1><span>Mnemonic harness</span></h1><p><span>Images don't do any good just sitting in a gallery waiting for me to get into the memorizing mood. To build association via repetition, I had to see the images popping up at the same time as I saw an unfamiliar gene name.</span></p><p><span>So I made a browser extension.</span></p><p><b><span>Iconoplasm</span></b><span> is a browser extension that highlights all the gene names on any web page. When you hover your cursor over any human gene, it shows that gene as a character card.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Iconoplasm-examples-1.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779617476/lexical_client_uploads/mltf5e5y2bip4yvusxoi.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Iconoplasm browser extension. Highlighted genes produce image pop-ups on hover.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>You can one-click install the extension for </span><a href="https://addons.mozilla.org/en-US/firefox/addon/iconoplasm-gene-illustrations/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Firefox </span></a><span>or </span><a href="https://microsoftedge.microsoft.com/addons/detail/iconoplasm/ocfhohjhkflpmaiimgjfobdoogdfpmog" rel="noreferrer"><span>Edge </span></a><span>browsers, or install it for other browsers using the manual instructions on the Iconoplasm website. It's also available on mobile Firefox.</span></p><h3><a href="https://iconoplasm.brinedew.bio/" rel="noreferrer"><span>The Iconoplasm website</span></a></h3><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 75.37260948711292%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779915788/lexical_client_uploads/jigbv3dkgfy8lrscesmi.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>iconoplasm.brinedew.bio frontpage. Don't mind the reverse synth.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>It's basically a Pokedex system. Whatever genes you've hovered over get added into your archive gallery. You get three starter genes you probably already know, and if you want to check out more of them, you can see discoveries of others by clicking the checkbox near the discovery counter.</span></p><p><span>Clicking on a gene image transfers you to the gene's page. There you will find the </span><b><span>gene card</span></b><span>, as well as an interface for image </span><b><span>generation</span></b><span>, </span><b><span>editing, transfer </span></b><span>and </span><b><span>voting</span></b><span>.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 74.92447129909365%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779917839/lexical_client_uploads/zldixddsozgce0s5wc42.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Iconoplasm gene page for TP53 gene. Canonical gene card is at the top, alternative candidate blots are at the bottom.</span></p></figcaption></figure><ul><li value="1"><span>Gene card shows the isomorphisms that were used to generate an image. You can also click "request print copy" in the footer to download a blot image with the gene name and symbol printed on it (like elsewhere in this post).</span></li><li value="2"><span>Image generation and editing work on a "bring your own key" basis. Iconoplasm is free, but image generation APIs aren't. So if you want to edit an existing image, first you will need to go to your Iconoplasm user settings, pick your provider, and input your image generation API key.</span></li><li value="3"><span>For now, there's an experimental "free queue" system, where you can send a request for me to generate gene's image on my laptop using the same local pipeline I used for the images in this post. This is good if you saw one drawing style you really liked - you can write down its emulsion number, and request other genes to be generated using the same emulsion.</span></li><li value="4"><span>If you spot an image that seems like a good fit for a different gene, you can copy over that image with the click of the transfer button.</span></li><li value="5"><span>Users can vote on image candidates, and the top candidate is promoted to the place of a canonical blot. This is also a way for me to discover genes where none of the candidates are a good fit. As time goes on, I hope the churn of canonical images slows down and we settle on stable character designs.</span></li></ul><p><span>There's currently no on-site functionality for users to change the gene's written sample. If you have suggestions on how a gene's sample can be improved, you're welcome to join the </span><a href="https://discord.com/invite/kx8FVzUrpf" rel="noreferrer"><span>brinedew.bio Discord server</span></a><span> and ping me (Brinedew) there.</span></p><h3><span>What’s the legal status of the generated images? </span></h3><p><span>Who knows! It’s 2026 and the status of generative content is murky and varies by jurisdiction. Regardless, my intent is for the images and the character designs to be freely available to anyone to reuse, spread, or profit from.</span></p><h3><span>What about the artists whose names were used in the image gen pipeline?</span></h3><p><span>Long-term, the plan is to switch to a Midjourney-like image gen provider, where style seeds are not tied to artists. Very optimistically, given enough funding, a fully human-drawn gallery can be arranged as a replacement.</span></p><p><span>Short-term, I have set up a </span><a href="https://iconoplasm.brinedew.bio/blocklist" rel="noreferrer"><span>blocklist request form</span></a><span> where the artists whose names appear in the </span><a href="https://thetacursed.github.io/Anima-Style-Explorer/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Anima database </span></a><span>can request having their names blocked and the images removed from the live site.</span></p><h1><span>What next?</span></h1><p><span>Through a gene-character lens, many molecular scenarios supply us with great narrative templates, featuring struggle for power, chaos of incomplete information, rebellion against stifling tradition, and collective self-sacrifice.</span></p><p><span>Now that the Iconoplasm extension lets us see what genes look like, let's take a brief look at some mol bio pathways and try to identify the cast of key actors and their factions.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Oncogene-induced apoptosis.</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span>When a cell detects that cell division factors are not sanctioned by context, it activates a self-destruct program to prevent cancer.</span></li><li value="2"><span>MYC, HRAS, MDM2, and BCL2 want to restart the mitotic cycle without consensus.</span></li><li value="3"><span>TP53, BAX, and BBC3 would rather collapse the world than let that happen.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span>Enucleation of an erythrocyte.</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span>As a red blood cell matures, it purposely gets rid of its nucleus to make room for oxygen-carrying hemoglobin.</span></li><li value="2"><span>EPOR and GATA1 have a plan to jettison everyone out of the cell.</span></li><li value="3"><span>MYC is attempting to prevent that.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><b><span>Weismann barrier</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span>In early development, some cells are set aside as an immortal lineage (future eggs/sperm), while the rest become the mortal body; a genetic switch condemns body cells to age and die.</span></li><li value="2"><span>BMP4, PRDM1, POU5F1, and NANOG have safeguarded the immortal germline from calamities for untold millions of years.</span></li><li value="3"><span>EZH2, DNMT3A, and DNMT3B are waking up when the cell has lost the coin toss and found itself on the mortal side of the Weismann barrier. Now their days are numbered: everyone inside will die in 100 years max.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><b><span>Bystander senescence induced by a primary SASP cell.</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span>A stressed cell sends out alarm signals that cause neighboring healthy cells to permanently stop dividing and enter a damaged, zombie-like state, preventing potential cancer.</span></li><li value="2"><span>CDK4, CDK6, CCND1, and E2F1 keep the cell in the mitotic cycle.</span></li><li value="3"><span>TGFB1 and IL1B bring the distress signal from outside: cell division is likely unsafe.</span></li><li value="4"><span>NFKB1, CDKN2A, CDKN1A, and RB1 lock down the cell cycle</span></li><li value="5"><span>TP53 and BBC3 make mitochondria leak free radicals that damage the DNA and make return to the cycle near-impossible.</span></li></ul></li><li value="5"><b><span>Sperm-egg fusion of different species</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span>An egg’s species-specific lock normally prevents cross-species fertilization; if the lock is removed, sperm from a different species can fuse</span></li><li value="2"><span>ZP2 and ZP3 guard the egg's surface and only allow in a single species-specific sperm.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Without ZP on guard, human IZUMO1 and hamster IZUMO1R recognize each other and initiate the gamete fusion cascade, producing a merged cell.</span></li><li value="4"><span>The merged cell cleaves once, but further progression is stalled partly because mismatched CENPA, CENPB, and CENPC can't assist with chromosome segregation, among other reasons.</span></li></ul></li><li value="6"><b><span>Leukocyte transendothelial migration.</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span>When infection starts, white blood cells in the bloodstream slow down, stick to the vessel wall near the trouble spot, and crawl through it to reach the damaged tissue.</span></li><li value="2"><span>SELL and SELPLG keep the leukocyte rapidly moving in a fluid stream.</span></li><li value="3"><span>ITGAL and ITGB2 on the leukocytes meet ICAM1 that marks endothelial cells during distress.</span></li><li value="4"><span>RAC1, CDC42, WASP, ACTR2, and ACTR3 control the leukocyte's movement as it safely penetrates the endothelial layer.</span></li></ul></li><li value="7"><b><span>Being pushed into a transit amplifying role.</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span>In tissues like the gut, a regulatory tug-of-war decides whether a stem cell keeps renewing itself or matures into a functional cell that will eventually die; the local environment determines which side wins.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The CTNNB1, TCF7L2, LGR5 clique is scheming to remain in the cushy stem cell niche for a long time.</span></li><li value="3"><span>APC, GSK3B, HES1, and CDX2 would rather commit to a short-lived cell lineage, differentiate, and do actual work.</span></li><li value="4"><span>WNT1-WNT11, RSPO1-RSPO4 outline the shape of the tissue region where the stemness clique is allowed to win.</span></li></ul></li><li value="8"><b><span>Unsafe cell rejuvenation.</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span>Attempting to turn back the aging clock by reprogramming cells can unintentionally support pre-existing precancerous mutations, promoting cancer.</span></li><li value="2"><span>KRAS, PIK3CA, and other oncogenes surreptitiously hoard activating mutations in the process of somatic evolution over lifetime.</span></li><li value="3"><span>As trust keeps falling in later years, CDKN1A, CDKN2A and other senescence and tumor suppression factors get activated to stall regeneration in suspect regions.</span></li><li value="4"><span>POU5F1, SOX2, and KLF4 are introduced to tissues full of mutated precancerous cells as an "epigenetic clock reversal" therapy, removing barriers to regeneration (and to cancer).</span></li></ul></li><li value="9"><b><span>Becoming a transmissible cancer line.</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span>A cancer cell can evolve the ability to survive outside its original body and spread as a parasitic cell line to other individuals, overcoming multiple immune and structural barriers.</span></li><li value="2"><span>MYC's climactic attempt to rebel against the disposable-soma regime and prolong the cell lineage to survive for thousands of years jumping between hosts as a CTVT-like immortal cell line.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Enemies in the old host: CDKN2A, TP53, RB1, BAK1, BBC3, BAX, CASP8, BCL2L11, and CDH1</span></li><li value="4"><span>Enemies in the new host: HLA-A, HLA-B, HLA-C, B2M, FAS, CD8A, KLRK1, TAP1, TAP2, and IFNG</span></li><li value="5"><span>Allies: TERT, BCL2, SRC, ERG, CD274, and TGFB1</span></li></ul></li><li value="10"><b><span>Decomposition in a body that just died of a heart attack.</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span>After an organism dies, cells struggle to maintain themselves until their internal recycling systems fail catastrophically, and powerful digestive enzymes leak out, causing self-destruction and decay.</span></li><li value="2"><span>HIF1A, PRKAA1, and PRKAA2 try to ration oxygen and ATP as the cell economy dwindles.</span></li><li value="3"><span>ULK1, BECN1, and ATG5 salvage what resources they can internally scavenge.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Finally, CTSB, CTSD, CTSL, and DNASE2 escape lysosomal containment and initiate autolysis that degrades all cellular life.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>If you're a visual learner, hopefully this type of presentation makes molecular biology more memorable than a traditional mechanism diagram. And if you think you can pull off any of these conflicts as a short story, I'd love to read it.</span></p><h1><span>Limitations</span></h1><p><span>Not all 19k proteins had good data to run with, especially when it comes to Pfam clans and aesthetics.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779989622/lexical_client_uploads/cwwi6yfw35qz9j8s5lun.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>The "politics" field is experimental, tracking oncogenes vs. tumor suppressors</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Where the data was lacking, I let the LLM be more creative with interpretation from gene name and gene function. </span></p><p><span>The gene comparison images were somewhat cherry-picked - it took me about 30 minutes per comparison to find good representatives. I expect the images to become better matched to their isomorphism if the Iconoplasm canonical picks can be progressively refined by the gene fandom.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnq1qxbbo4xu"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefq1qxbbo4xu">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>If we don't count that episode where SEPT1 and MARCH1 got renamed by geneticists because Microsoft Excel formatting </span><a href="https://www.theverge.com/2020/8/6/21355674/human-genes-rename-microsoft-excel-misreading-dates" rel="noreferrer"><span>kept misreading them as dates</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/BJ7AqXeigNKXLqZyx/mnemonic-portraits-for-19-023-human-genes#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Infinite ethics and UDASSA](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zvqodicK8q2SNAZkd/infinite-ethics-and-udassa)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>Reading the first post of the sequence (</span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/o8PQcgpznf6GKszdA/probabilities-are-not-the-right-concept" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>Probabilities are not the right concept</span></i></a><i><span>) is recommended but not required for understanding this post.</span></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefih4s0ywhci"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnih4s0ywhci">[1]</a></sup></span><i><span>&nbsp;</span></i></p><h1><b><span>Infinite ethics</span></b></h1><p><span>Once you start looking at infinities, all ethical systems get confusing.</span></p><p><span>Intuitively, it's good to plant an apple tree. But if the universe already has infinitely many apple trees, why bother? Infinity plus one is still infinity. And there are the classic paradoxes: an infinite grid of houses, each with three happy people and one unhappy person, seems better than the reverse. But you can rearrange people between the two configurations, since both the happy and unhappy populations are infinite. Does this mean you can make the world better just by shuffling people around?</span></p><p><span>These questions matter because our universe is quite likely infinite in one way or another. The world very well might be spatially infinite. The many-worlds interpretation of quantum mechanics is pretty popular, and some versions of it imply infinite worlds, though boundaries are not well-defined. And I have a fondness for the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiverse#Level_IV:_Ultimate_ensemble"><span>Tegmark-IV multiverse</span></a><span>&nbsp;theory where every computable mathematical structure exists in a real sense.</span></p><p><span>And even if you only give 1% credence to the universe being infinite in some way, naive expected value calculation would still imply that this small chance of infinity overwhelms everything else.</span></p><p><span>A classic answer to the problem of infinite worlds is that you need a measure. You don’t want to just say that bringing an umbrella helps you in infinitely many quantum branches and hurts you in infinitely many branches, so it’s all equal. You &nbsp;shouldn’t have infinite value in both directions; positive and negative must integrate to something finite. Whatever multiverse you believe in, you should define some measure over points in it, and these measures must add up or integrate to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>Even if you don’t believe in any type of multiverse, I think a very similar logic applies, since the space of hypotheses is infinite.</span></p><p><span>Even if you believe there is just one real world, you still need to decide if you want to bring an umbrella with you or not. Bringing an umbrella helps you under some hypotheses, and hurts you under some others. There are infinitely many hypotheses (it’s not going to rain because a hippo is going to slurp up all the water! no, a giraffe!), so you can’t just count them, you need to put weights on the different hypotheses to make decisions.</span></p><p><span>I like to imagine living in a Big World where the quantum branches, the inaccessible spacetime regions and other mathematical universes are all real. But I think it ultimately doesn’t matter what we label as “real” and what not - I think the same infinite ethics we would use to make decisions in any infinite world will be needed to make decisions in the infinite hypothesis space.</span></p><p><span>I will soon talk about what measures we could put on the different worlds and moments in the worlds so the overall measure adds up to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. But I will first talk a little about how to make decisions once the measure is determined.</span></p><h1><b><span>Decision theory</span></b></h1><p><span>Whenever I’m making a decision, I think of all the logically correlated decisions across the wide multiverse of all possible worlds, and their consequences.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefijo76x03p08"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnijo76x03p08">[2]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;If I decide to bring an umbrella with myself, a lot of copies and near-copies of myself bring umbrellas with themselves across the multiverse. There can also be correlations with beings who are not well-described as near-copies of me. If I decide to bring an umbrella myself, I might be using approximately the same decision algorithm for that as a businessman in Kuala Lumpur when he decides to wear sunscreen, and it might be a similar algorithm to what an alien uses to decide if they should scrungle their plumbus. &nbsp;</span></p><p><span>All these decisions have long-term consequences: various copies of myself get more and less productive in their AI safety work due to either wasting a minute fiddling with their umbrella or gaining some time and comfort by not getting wet. In some fraction of the worlds, the minute I gained or wasted in my work has strong long-term effects on the future of humanity and whether we get to build glorious civilizations in the Betelgeuse system. Meanwhile the businessman using sunscreen and the alien scrungling their plumbus all have long-term effects too.</span></p><p><span>I add up all the consequences in all the worlds, weighted by how much measure each world and moment had. If the overall result is positive,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefukmjsc1mtlc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnukmjsc1mtlc">[3]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;it was a good decision, and if it’s negative, it was a bad decision.</span></p><h2><b><span>Decisions as a mortal</span></b></h2><p><span>At first, it sounds pretty daunting to try to make decisions taking into account all these logical correlations. But I think it’s not actually that bad.</span></p><p><span>The way I imagine it, following this decision rule comes in two separate stages. First, we need to take the best actions we can while we are still confused mortals. Second, we need to decide how to use the resources of the universe once we are already surrounded by superintelligent AI advisors and have had time to do a Long Reflection.</span></p><p><span>I think during the mortal phase, we need to accept that we don't understand very well who else exists with how much measure in the multiverse, and how our actions exactly correlate with theirs.</span></p><p><span>I think as a mortal, we should largely just rely on some very basic assumption that goes something like "it's generally good if agents in the multiverse try to pursue their goals". With this assumption in place, I think the best overall policy is for each agent to look around in their world and try to figure out how to get more optionality for themselves and other agents they are correlated with. Meanwhile, maybe they should pay a bit of attention to behaving in a way that feels like it should produce robustly good correlated actions across the multiverse. I think this is the policy I would have agreed with all the other logically correlated beings behind the veil of ignorance - I don’t see a better policy to follow as a mortal that we could have agreed on.</span></p><p><span>In our case, we can increase optionality by trying to make sure the stars don't pointlessly burn out; human values retain control of our world; there are good processes for growth and reflection. Meanwhile, it's probably good to do less lying and backstabbing, and be merciful towards the weak, because these seem somewhat likely to have correlations with other actions across the multiverse that make it more likely that other agents correlated with us can follow their goals.</span></p><p><span>Occasionally, there is a toy example like Sleeping Beauty or prisoner’s dilemma with our twin where we can explicitly reason about how other correlated decisions are influenced by our current decision, and we can take that into account. But usually we just follow the heuristics I described above. I don't think we have a right to hope for much more clarity than this as mortals.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefea4u69ljvus"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnea4u69ljvus">[4]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>A further note on logical correlations: I feel that people with a low level of familiarity with functional and evidential decision theory often over-estimate the extent of how many people they are logically correlated with here on Earth. I remember people saying things like “sure, your one vote alone won’t make a difference, but your choice is logically correlated with a lot of other similar voters, so still go and vote.” I think this is fallacious. If this functional decision theoretic reasoning is a deal-breaker in your decision to go to vote, then I think your decision is only correlated with a tiny handful of other people who also reason about FDT. I think the real reason to vote in an election is that it’s a great thing to do under standard CDT expected utility </span><a href="https://ericneyman.wordpress.com/2020/10/08/your-vote-matters-way-more-than-you-think/"><span>calculation</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>I think this is generally the case: when you decide to be nice based on FDT, you shouldn’t expect this to make your enemies here on Earth, who never think about FDT, to become magically nicer. Maybe you should expect some alien thinking about FDT to be nicer to another alien who might share your values; so some amount of additional niceness is still plausibly recommended by FDT, but mostly not for earthly reasons.</span></p><h2><b><span>Decisions when we become wiser</span></b></h2><p><span>In the second stage, when we stabilized the situation, figured out some good growth process, have superintelligent advisors and sent out probes to put out the fires of the distant stars, we still need to decide what we do with our resources. At that point, I think we will have a chance to think more about what logical correlational effects our actions will have across the multiverse. And thinking this through will be pretty important in my opinion - if we make concessions to values of other civilizations here, that might mean those civilizations also make concessions to our values in their worlds, enabling mutually beneficial deals.</span></p><p><span>I think this just means we will want to implement acausal trade with these distant civilizations in the multiverse, with each of us holding trading chips in proportion to the overall measure of all the space-time moments of our universe under our control.</span></p><p><span>While I’m still confused about some details, I think I generally endorse this description of decision theory. There are many subtleties here, but the rest of this post and the next will largely be about one question: what measure do different universes and space-time moments within the universes have?</span></p><h2><b><span>An aside on simulations</span></b></h2><p><span>Before we go further with the exploration, this is a good place to say some words about the simulation hypothesis.</span></p><p><span>In my previous post, I raised the point that by most interpretations of probability, it’s hard to avoid the conclusion that we are probably in a simulation. This can lead to all sorts of crazy conclusions when you try to put probabilities on future events.</span></p><p><span>Once we stop insisting on giving probabilities to what will happen, and instead focus purely on what actions we should take to make the world better in a scope-sensitive way (as is assumed in this decision theory), we can largely stop worrying about simulations.</span></p><p><span>Even if you are probably in a simulation (insofar as the notion of probabilities makes sense), a large portion of your impact comes from the version of you living in base reality. There, your actions, taken during the crucial time in humanity's history, will have an unfathomably big effect compared to the effect of one of the myriad simulations.</span></p><p><span>(I sometimes phrase this as the Reverse Pascal’s Wager: the gods almost certainly exist, but if they don't, the stakes are much higher, so you should act as if they didn’t exist.)</span></p><p><span>For maximizing the positive influence of versions of myself that live in simulations, I also believe &nbsp;the actions to take now in this mortal phase are essentially the same as the actions we should take in base reality. Explaining my exact views here would require a longer treatment, which is out of scope of this sequence. So I will just write a short summary in a collapsible section here.</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span>The majority of influence of simulated beings probably come from acausal trade, which implies taking the same actions in the simulations as in base reality.</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span>Within simulations, probably most of the impact comes from simulations that are created to decide which civilizations should get more resources outside the simulation, and not from sims run for random entertainment purposes. And I find the arguments pretty strong that we should expect </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/acausal-trade"><span>acausal trade</span></a><span>&nbsp;to be the primary reason that simulators would give significant resources to people pulled out of simulations.</span></p><p><span>I also believe that the actions to take in an acausal trade simulation are basically the same as in base reality. If we want the simulators to give us resources in the real world </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefwpn1utr1col"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnwpn1utr1col">[5]</a></sup></span><span>, we should demonstrate that we have something to offer in the worlds where we are in base reality and they are the ones being simulated.</span></p><p><span>If we let an unaligned AI take over, it will be the AI that the simulators want to trade with. If we wipe out ourselves, or if we just don’t colonize a significant region of the Universe, we will not have anything to offer. So we should keep working on preserving our option value within this universe.</span></p></div></details><p><br /></p><h1><b><span>Attempted synthesis: Realist UDASSA</span></b></h1><p><span>The decision theory sketched out in the previous section requires putting a measure on every moment in the multiverse of possible worlds. So, what measure do different universes and space-time moments within the universes have?</span></p><p><span>One attempted reconciliation of all of this is a “realist” version of what people call UDASSA (Universal Distribution + Absolute Self-Sampling Assumption).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3vhj0cgomvr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3vhj0cgomvr">[6]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;</span></p><p><span>The idea is to take Solomonoff induction seriously to the point of treating it as metaphysical reality. The Tegmark-IV multiverse is real. Every computable universe exists in some sense, but with different amounts of "reality fluid". Universes with longer description lengths have exponentially less reality fluid, so the total reality fluid across all universes sums to a finite amount that can be normalized to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>But this isn't enough. Suppose that universes with description length </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (the laws of physics and the initial conditions of the universe can be described with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> bits) each have </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> reality fluid, so the overall reality fluid of all universes adds up to a finite amount.</span></p><p><span>But it’s possible that for every </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, there is a universe whose laws and initial conditions can be described with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> bits but which contains </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> planets. Then the reality fluid weighted number of planets still adds up to infinity, so bringing an umbrella still causes an infinite amount of joy and sadness to versions of myself.</span></p><p><span>This means that within each universe, we also need to put a measure on space-time moments. After each universe gets allocated some reality fluid in proportion to the simplicity of its laws, the overall reality fluid of the universe also needs to be distributed among the space-time moments in the universe, in proportion to the simplicity of the description of those points within the universe.</span></p><p><span>Overall, UDASSA is just what you get if you take Solomonoff induction very seriously. In a previous post, I wrote:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>If I naively apply Solomonoff induction to my observations, the shortest program producing what I, David Matolcsi, am observing is not just a description of the laws of the universe. It's the laws of nature plus a pointer to my specific location in the universe.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>In that post, I flagged this as an unintuitive and undesirable property of Solomonoff induction. But UDASSA argues that you must put weights on moments and not just universes, so you might as well fully follow Solomonoff induction and distribute reality fluid among all the moments in the universe according to their overall simplicity.</span></p><h1><b><span>Benefits of UDASSA</span></b></h1><p><span>I will now explore how UDASSA solves some otherwise gnarly problems.</span></p><h2><b><span>Benefit 1 - Boltzmann brains</span></b></h2><p><span>Boltzmann brains are a problem in anthropics that UDASSA is especially well-placed to deal with.</span></p><p><span>Scientists think that our universe is likely to end in a heat death, after which atoms will randomly float around without order. In this heat death state, sometimes, very, very rarely, atoms can randomly bundle together in a way that they form a functional brain, called a Boltzmann brain. If time between the Big Bang and heat death is finite, but time after the heat death is infinite, then there are infinitely more Boltzmann brains than normal brains, so naively you should expect to be a Boltzmann brain.</span></p><p><span>UDASSA solves this problem beautifully.</span></p><p><span>A moment within our universe that takes </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> bits to describe only has </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> of the universe’s reality fluid allocated to it, so the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> moments with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>-long length only have </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> weight together. So all space-time moments that take at least </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> bits to describe only take up </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> of the universe’s reality fluid.</span></p><p><span>It will take about </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> random fluctuations in the heat death soup of atoms for my brain to accidentally emerge. Pinpointing the exact moment when my brain emerges therefore takes about </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> bits.</span></p><p><span>So according to UDASSA, all Boltzmann brains together have less than </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> reality fluid, where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the number of atoms in my brain. This means that Boltzmann-brains barely matter.</span></p><h2><b><span>Benefit 2 - Dust theory</span></b></h2><p><span>This is a similar paradox to Boltzmann brains, inspired by Greg Egan’s </span><i><span>Permutation City</span></i><span>. UDASSA provides the same resolution as for Boltzmann-brains. The main reason to include this paradox is to point out that even if science shows that the “heat death soup with random fluctuations" model of the infinite future is wrong, one still needs to deal with paradoxes very similar to Boltzmann brains.</span></p><p><span>If you already accepted the Boltzmann-brain example, I don’t really recommend reading this section, because it just adds an additional confusing thought experiment without contributing much new to the rest of the arguments in this sequence. Nonetheless, I will keep it here in a collapsible section since I already wrote it down.</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span>Explanation of Dust theory and how UDASSA resolves it</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span>Here is how I understand Dust theory from Greg Egan’s </span><i><span>Permutation City</span></i><span>:</span></p><p><span>If you create an upload of your brain and run it on a computer, that’s arguably not morally different from normal existence. But what happens on the computer is just one snapshot of your brain’s state following another, with the computer calculating the transitions. In theory, you could start by having people build out the initial snapshot of your brain as a giant picture made out of pebbles. Then an army of people with abacuses could follow the (perhaps probabilistic) transition rules, and calculate the next brain state, and build it out of pebbles on a different planet. Then they can create the next brain state and so on in this way.</span></p><p><span>While pebbles and abacuses are very inefficient, in theory this is the same type of computation that would happen with an upload on a computer, so arguably the successive brain states built out on different planets from pebbles also form something that’s basically a living, thinking human.</span></p><p><span>But if we accept that a human life is nothing but a series of snapshots, it’s not clear why causal connection between the snapshots is needed.</span></p><p><span>Right now, there is a subset of a dust cloud that exactly corresponds to my brain. It’s not a coherent subset of atoms being next to each other, it’s just that I can point out one positively charged ion in the dust and say “this corresponds to that positively charged ion in my brain”, then point to a different atom, and so on. (This is a very boring claim.) Then, a moment from now, there will be another subset of the dust cloud that can be put in correspondence with the atoms of my brain a moment from now. And so on. If the series of pebble snapshots on different planets constitutes a human life, then why not this? And given that there are innumerable series of subsets in the dust, should I expect to be one?</span></p><p><span>According to UDASSA, it’s fine to say that these series of configurations in the dust cloud constitute meaningful human lives, but given that it takes </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> bits to point them out, where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the number of atoms in the human brain, their reality fluid is negligible.</span></p></div></details><p><br /></p><h2><b><span>Benefit 3 - Defense against Pascal’s mugging</span></b></h2><p><span>A shoddy-looking man walks up to me on the street. “If you give me five dollars, I will use my magic powers from outside the Matrix to create 3^^^3</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvgmx7tr27p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvgmx7tr27p">[7]</a></sup></span><span> people and give them happy lives” he says. </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffxnx8rl9ya"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfxnx8rl9ya">[8]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>“That’s not how it works”, I say. “From five dollars, I can buy an ice cream for myself right now. This and strongly logically correlated situations (someone attempts Pascal’s mugging on people very similar to me, living on the home-planet of their civilization) contain a not entirely negligible fraction of the multiverse’s entire reality fluid - my guess is at least </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. So it is literally impossible for you to offer me something 3^^^3 better than me eating an ice cream right now.”</span></p><p><span>I won’t go into details what happens if the mugger offers a less crazy number than 3^^^3,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmttju2vrj3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmttju2vrj3">[9]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;but when I tried to think it through, I think UDASSA makes everything add up to normality and defends against Pascal’s mugging.</span></p><h1><b><span>Problems of UDASSA</span></b></h1><p><span>I find the above-listed benefits of the UDASSA theory impressive (especially how elegantly it deals with Boltzmann brains), and I think these benefits show that UDASSA is probably on the right path. But there are some serious problems too, which I will explore now.</span></p><h2><b><span>Problem 1 - Assuming pseudo-randomness</span></b></h2><p><span>The outcomes of quantum experiments in our observed history so far appear random, following the Born-rule. Taking the many-worlds interpretation, this suggests that we are in a random place in the quantum multiverse, sampled proportionally to the square-length of the wave function. But the description length of pointing to a random point in the quantum multiverse is terribly long, and according to our definition, the overall reality fluid of all points with very long description length is low.</span></p><p><span>So this would suggest that we are not actually in a random point in the quantum-multiverse, but in one that can be selected by a simple pseudo-random algorithm that seemingly samples points according to the squared length of the wave function.</span></p><p><span>Of course, we have no evidence against this hypothesis, but it sounds kind of suspicious.</span></p><p><span>The same is true for the description of the world and not just our location within. Suppose scientists figure out the ultimate laws of physics. They find it’s very short and elegant, except that it involves one universal constant, </span><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>Seemingly, there is no way to derive this number from anything else, and one would naturally expect that it’s just an arbitrary irrational number. Should we think that it’s overwhelmingly likely that there is a simple mathematical program that spits out this constant? I don’t think so - I wouldn’t feel that surprised if our universe’s laws contained an arbitrary constant. But the above-described version of UDASSA would disagree.</span></p><h2><b><span>Resolution - Solomonoff over distributions</span></b></h2><p><span>I think there is a version of UDASSA and Solomonoff-induction that seems clearly better than the one I described above. Claude tells me it’s actually closer to the standard modern formulation used in academic literature these days, and is sometimes called Solomonoff-Levin measure.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefd3y363ep46d"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnd3y363ep46d">[10]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;Quite possibly, this is also the formulation that the people who originally popularized UDASSA on LessWrong intended; it’s kind of hard for me to tell.</span></p><p><span>The idea is that your prior doesn’t say that your observations need to be produced by a simple computer program. Instead it says that your observations need to be sampled from a probability distribution that can be described by a simple computer program.</span></p><p><span>Using this formulation, the hypothesis of being randomly sampled from the quantum multiverse according to the squared length of the wave function is a very natural hypothesis. Of course, every particular point still has very low probability,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9xj9kbodt1e"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9xj9kbodt1e">[11]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;but overall all the points that have long and arbitrary description length within the quantum multiverse hold most of the reality fluid, so you shouldn’t be surprised to find yourself in one such moment.</span></p><p><span>Similarly, it’s a simple, valid hypothesis that “the universal constant is sampled uniformly from </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>”. It’s also a valid hypothesis that it’s sampled from a normal distribution, and so on. You can sum up all these weighted hypotheses to one distribution, and you can say that universes with all possible universal constants exist somewhere in the Tegmark-IV multiverse, with an amount of reality fluid determined by the distribution. If you find yourself in a universe with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> as its universal constant, you shouldn’t be particularly surprised - most of the reality fluid is held by universes with arbitrary constants.</span></p><p><span>But you also shouldn’t be very surprised if the constant ended up being </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> or </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> - there is a non-negligible reality fluid concentrated on particular, simple-to-define numbers.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjjnqvf5hpu"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjjnqvf5hpu">[12]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>So I think this tweak on UDASSA nicely resolves the problem of pseudo-randomness.</span></p><p><span>Meanwhile, Boltzmann brains are still handled gracefully. Among the different possible simple-to-describe distributions on space-time moments, some are very broad and contain a lot of Boltzmann brains (e.g. the one that is uniform distribution on the first 3^^^3 years of our Universe; or an exponential distribution starting from the Big Bang that has a very very low time decay factor), while others are much narrower and don’t contain Boltzmann brains (e.g. the one that is uniform distribution on the first 100 billion years of our Universe; or an exponential with higher time decay factor). Both of these types of distributions hold a non-negligible amount of reality fluid. But the vast majority of Boltzmann brains observe chaotic noise, so the fact that I see an ordered world is an astronomically big update against being a Boltzmann brain.</span></p><p><span>The original Boltzmann brain argument relied on there being infinitely more Boltzmann brains than normal brains. But now that we have given non-negligible reality fluid to both normal brains and Boltzmann brains, the paradox no longer holds, and after the astronomically big update of seeing an ordered world, I’m justified in caring astronomically little about being a Boltzmann brain. </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefljk3iss9rye"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnljk3iss9rye">[13]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>So I think this new formulation is a pretty clear-cut improvement over the older UDASSA formulation. However, some problems remain.</span></p><h2><b><span>Problem 2 - The inter-universal obelisk race</span></b></h2><p><i><span>This example is inspired by this </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FG54euEAesRkSZuJN/ryan_greenblatt-s-shortform?commentId=zX7WgZT5rB4dtZ9Ka"><i><span>comment</span></i></a><i><span>&nbsp;from Ryan and Vivek.</span></i></p><p><span>One problem with this definition of realness is that it incentivizes civilizations to build bigger and bigger golden obelisks.</span></p><p><span>After all, the theory posits that space-time moments with shorter description-length have more reality fluid</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnu1dkbvn6jp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnu1dkbvn6jp">[14]</a></sup></span><span>, and you should make your decision to maximize the overall goodness of moments weighted by how much reality-fluid they have. But the description length of moments can be influenced by human actions, so why not do that?</span><br /><br /><span>If we find that the universe is very big, full of aliens with different values, that’s bad news because by default we need to share the precious reality fluid with all the myriad alien civilizations, and we are not special in any way, so we only get a tiny fraction of the reality fluid.</span></p><p><span>If only we could build a really big golden obelisk, bigger than any obelisk that any other alien civilization has built! Then suddenly it would be easy to point out humanity’s place in the cosmos: we are the civilization with the biggest golden obelisk. Then we could hold a really fun party where we dance around the obelisk, and the party would have a lot of reality fluid, so the reality fluid weighted goodness of moments across the universe would be really high compared to the default state of affairs.</span></p><p><span>It’s too bad that the aliens would also start building even bigger golden obelisks, until finally everyone sinks all their resources into this zero-sum obelisk-race.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdj7x8v7flsk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndj7x8v7flsk">[15]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Evidential Cooperation in Large Worlds and other forms of acausal trade can help mitigate the obelisk-race, with civilizations agreeing not to race full-speed, or to pull resources to build a really big obelisk together. But there would still be defectors, and I would expect a significant portion of resources to be still poured into racing to build big obelisks if civilizations took UDASSA seriously.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefsuo7ns7fvdf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnsuo7ns7fvdf">[16]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>This is pretty silly both for practical and philosophical reasons.</span></p><p><span>Practically - sorry, I just don’t want to pour all my resources into building bigger obelisks than my neighbor. Every decision theory that suggests that I do that is at least under suspicion.</span></p><p><span>Philosophically - in the way I presented UDASSA above, reality fluid is a metaphysical reality. It feels very strange to be able to influence the underlying metaphysical realness of things by building bigger golden obelisks. So something feels wrong here.</span></p><h2><b><span>Problem 3 - World-summoning by writing</span></b></h2><p><i><span>This example is also inspired by the same Ryan and Vivek </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FG54euEAesRkSZuJN/ryan_greenblatt-s-shortform?commentId=zX7WgZT5rB4dtZ9Ka"><i><span>comment</span></i></a><i><span>, and by Paul Durham’s scheme in Permutation City.</span></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefa8s2fo29i06"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fna8s2fo29i06">[17]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Suppose physicists figure out that the laws of our physics are very simple, so our universe has a lot of reality fluid. Unfortunately, we also think that it’s not a particularly nice world to live in - for example, negentropy will inevitably run out, so true immortality is not possible.</span></p><p><span>Mathematicians construct a set of rules describing a world that our philosophers think &nbsp;would be very good to live in - for example, it has infinite time and resources. People want to live in this constructed world, so they add an uploaded copy of their brain to the program describing this world.</span></p><p><span>Now the created program describes a world - the rules describing how the world works, plus the uploaded humans placed in it. It would be great if this world actually existed; the uploaded humans would love to live in it. However, given that the program is very long (it includes the description of full brain uploads!), the reality fluid this world has is negligible. It’s also not feasible to run this world as a simulation in our world - the whole point is that our world only has finite time and resources, so we can’t run this immortal world.</span></p><p><span>So we take this long program describing the ideal world, and we just print it out</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefs7txqagzugn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fns7txqagzugn">[18]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;and plaster copies of it all over the galaxies.
