Thus, the description length of the program will become fairly short - our universe’s description length was short, and much of our Universe is covered by copies of this text, so if we get lucky enough to plaster the text in a short description length location, there will be a short pointer to the program.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefyj1k30mqo5f"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnyj1k30mqo5f">[19]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;(And in any case, it will be the most common piece of text in the Universe, and “most common piece of text” is not </span><i><span>that</span></i><span>&nbsp;hard to point out.)</span></p><p><span>So now this complicated immortal universe with our uploaded brains inserted in it has a fairly short description length, so it exists with a significant amount of realness.</span></p><p><span>I think this is very silly.</span></p><h2><b><span>Problem 4 - I just don’t believe in reality fluid</span></b></h2><p><i><span>This objection is inspired by Joe Carlsmith’s incredulity in his own </span></i><a href="https://joecarlsmith.com/2021/11/28/anthropics-and-the-universal-distribution/"><i><span>post</span></i></a><i><span>&nbsp;on UDASSA, and by looking deep into my heart.</span></i></p><p><span>What is this reality fluid? Why would I believe that everything exists in a mathematical multiverse but with this particular prior making some points "exist more"? I don’t get what it means for some universes and points to have more of an existence.</span></p><p><span>Some people try to hold a middle-ground saying that they don’t believe in realist UDASSA in particular, but they believe in the existence of reality fluid, whose distribution we don’t know, but the Solomonoff-induction is the ignorance-prior we use as our best guess of the reality fluid’s distribution. I feel uncompelled by this too. What is this reality fluid and why should I believe it exists?</span></p><p><span>There is no feedback-loop; I can never get any possible evidence for how the reality fluid is distributed. If this particular space-time moment has a bunch of reality fluid, but the other side of my room has very little reality fluid, how would I possibly know that? The theory doesn’t posit that people can sense the amount of realness in any way - so if the other side of the room has very little reality fluid for some reason, I won’t notice it in any way when I walk over there.</span></p><p><span>The only possible evidence I have about the distribution of reality fluid is that I can update that the specific moment I am in right now maybe has relatively high reality fluid. But it’s pretty hard to draw useful conclusions from one point.</span></p><p><span>And importantly, I think that even updating on the realness of this one moment is not really a valid move either.</span></p><p><span>First of all, if there is someone living in a low reality fluid moment but influencing later high reality-fluid moments, I want that person to make good decisions. If that person makes incorrect updates based on the assumption that their particular moment has high reality-fluid, and then makes bad decisions because of this, that’s not good. This means that when I’m making scope-sensitive decisions about the future, I shouldn’t make updates from the assumption that this moment is high reality-fluid either.</span></p><p><span>Second, simulations confuse everything if you try to update on your moment having high reality fluid. It’s possible that the real distribution of reality fluid is primarily concentrated in worlds that have seven spatial dimensions. But maybe a powerful alien civilization in the seven-dimensional world believes for some reason that three-dimensional worlds have a lot of reality fluid, or they have some arbitrary preference for affecting changes in three-dimensional worlds. Then they would plausibly run a lot of simulations of three-dimensional worlds for acausal trade reasons, and we might be in one. So even if you wanted to update on what kind of moment we are in (which I think you shouldn’t), I think it would be more of an update about what kind of worlds and moments powerful simulators care about than about the real distribution of reality fluid.</span></p><p><span>Finally, even if reality fluid was real, it would still be ultimately my choice to decide which worlds and moments to care about. Given that I believe that morality is subjective, it's always a self-consistent choice to say that one’s utility function is such that they only care about worlds where there is a teapot floating in the asteroid belt.</span></p><p><span>If someone came to me saying that's their utility function, I would think that was silly. I would recommend they reflect a bit more on it - that they think more about philosophy, they meditate a little, they play with a toddler, and try to imagine if the importance of any of this would be affected by the teapot. But if they come back saying that no, they still believe that only the teapot in the asteroid belt can give life meaning, I can’t argue with that. I’m not a moral realist, different people can have different utility functions.</span></p><p><span>As a more serious example, in my next post I will write some arguments about weighing the importance of smaller and bigger worlds, which ultimately comes down to something similar to intuitions about total and average utilitarianism. I think that even if reality fluid exists, it’s ultimately a moral decision what weight we put on the wellbeing of different worlds when making decisions.</span></p><p><span>I find it a strange theory that there exists this thing called reality fluid, which we can’t observe and whose distribution we cannot get any possible evidence about, and that we should maximize welfare weighted by this unknowable distribution of reality fluid - except if we decide to weigh worlds differently, given that as moral non-realists we are allowed to have preferences over which worlds we care more about.</span></p><p><span>At this point, why not cut out the middle-man, get rid of the assumption of the existence of the reality fluid, and rely entirely on subjective preferences on the importance of different worlds and moments?</span></p><h1><b><span>Attempted synthesis: Non-Realist UDASSA</span></b></h1><p><span>One possible resolution is to use the UDASSA framework described above, but declare that the reality fluid is not real. The weights put on the different space-time moments in the different universes do not represent some metaphysical quality of how “real” each moment is - they just represent how much I care about each universe and each moment. The weights are only as real as morality is - ultimately, I’m the one who chooses them according to my moral intuitions.</span></p><p><span>The best description of this view I know of is Scott Garrabrant’s </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NvwJMQvfu9hbBdG6d/preferences-without-existence"><span>Preferences without Existence</span></a><span>. I strongly recommend reading Garrabrant’s post before progressing further with this piece. It’s not long, and expresses this non-realist UDASSA viewpoint very well.</span></p><p><i><span>[Waiting for the reader to read Garrabrant’s post. No, really, you should read it before going further.]</span></i></p><h2><b><span>Problems with non-realist UDASSA</span></b></h2><p><span>I’m sympathetic to Garrabrant’s position; I think it makes more sense than positing the existence of a metaphysical reality fluid.</span></p><p><span>I also find the position aesthetically satisfying, and unlike some people, I don’t find it terrifying or a cause for nihilism that “existence” is not really a meaningful concept. I agree with Garrabrant’s sentiment:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>A: Okay, it seems plausible, but kind of depressing to think that we do not exist.</span></p><p><span>B: Oh, I disagree! I am still a mind with free will, and I have the power to use that will to change my own little piece of mathematics — the output of my decision procedure. To me that feels incredibly beautiful, eternal, and important.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>However, I still disagree with some parts of Garrabrant’s description.</span></p><p><span>To me, one advantage of thinking of weights as preferences and not as metaphysical reality is that the weights no longer need to be very precisely and elegantly described in math. If the precise definition leads to an unintuitive conclusion (like people in a short description-length world plastering their favorite world’s equation everywhere, thus making that world short description-length too), I no longer need to run off the cliff with my original definition. </span></p><p><span>I can just say sorry, no, I don’t actually care significantly more about a universe just because its fundamental equation was plastered around the galaxies by some aliens. I can just choose the weights myself, so I can make qualitative judgements that decreasing a universe’s or a moment’s description length by intentionally copying equations or building obelisks doesn’t count. I can do that even if these qualitative judgements don’t fit a precise mathematical formula - I’m a free man and I can choose my own morality however I want.</span></p><p><span>Garrabrant’s piece doesn’t engage with this position - his post implies that he is fully biting the bullet on caring more about simpler-to-describe universes, and doesn’t indicate that he has carve-outs for this rule. I don’t know whether he still endorses that position, and what his opinion is about the case of someone plastering their favorite equation everywhere.</span></p><p><span>(Don’t we lose something by no longer grounding the decision process on a mathematical formalism? I think not really. As I said, I think we ultimately need to make moral judgments on what we care about anyway. And it’s not like the mathematical formalism was very useful in the original UDASSA framing for getting people to agree on the implications of how much weight each moment gets.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmed50hgnz1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmed50hgnz1">[20]</a></sup></span><span>)</span></p><p><span>More importantly, I just don’t feel it in my heart why I would love mathematically simple universes so much. And I feel even less in my heart that I care more about simple-to-describe moments (or more precisely, moments sampled from simple-to-describe distributions) within each universe.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefr2apahmxag"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnr2apahmxag">[21]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Yes, I care a little bit about mathematical simplicity - I’m a mathematician by training, and I find simplicity aesthetically compelling. But I don’t feel like mathematical simplicity is very unique among the things I care about.</span></p><p><span>Instead of saying that I care about the goodness of the worlds weighted by how simple mathematical laws describe them, I could choose totally different weightings. I could rank possible universes (and moments within them) by how dramatic they are.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftemcbrlxll"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntemcbrlxll">[22]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;Then I could give ½ weight to the most dramatic universe, ¼ to the second most dramatic, and so on, the weights adding up to 1. And I could say that I try to maximize the goodness of worlds weighted by these dramaticness-weights. To me, using dramaticness sounds approximately as compelling as using mathematical simplicity for the weighting.</span></p><p><span>In my next post, I will try to make sense of this and propose my own resolution which I currently feel tentatively satisfied with.</span></p><p><br /></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnih4s0ywhci"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefih4s0ywhci">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>For the avoidance of doubt: </span><i><b><span>The views and opinions of the author expressed herein are personal and do not necessarily reflect those of the European Commission or other EU institutions.</span></b></i></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnijo76x03p08"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefijo76x03p08">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I think the decision theory I’m describing and mostly endorsing here is updateless functional decision theory. I’m not sure however how evidential and functional decision theories are different once you already grant updatelessness, so I don’t intend to make any statement in the evidential vs functional debate.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnukmjsc1mtlc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefukmjsc1mtlc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This is why it’s important that the overall measure adds up to 1 - if the measure was infinite, probably both the positive and negative consequences would have infinite measure, and the result would be undefined.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnea4u69ljvus"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefea4u69ljvus">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It was important to assume though that "it's generally good if agents in the multiverse try to pursue their goals". Otherwise, I could assume that there is an Anti-Me somewhere in the multiverse who has an equal amount of measure as me and whose decision process is perfectly correlated with mine, but who has diametrically opposed values. If that was the case, that would lead to total decision-paralysis - if I ever decide to pick up a 5 dollar bill from the ground, Anti-Me also picks it up, and the overall contribution to my values is 0. But I don’t believe everyone has an equal measure opponent in the multiverse, and I believe it’s overall good for agents to pursue their goals, so I pursue mine.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnwpn1utr1col"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefwpn1utr1col">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>And if we want to repay them for having created us, in a Parfit’s Hitchhiker way</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn3vhj0cgomvr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref3vhj0cgomvr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>At least what I understand UDASSA to mean based on a few descriptions I read and in-person conversations. It’s possible that some people mean something somewhat different by the word - as far as I can tell, the term emerged in some half-lost threads on a message board, and as far as I know, there is no single canonical explainer of the concept.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvgmx7tr27p"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvgmx7tr27p">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>A really unfathomably large </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knuth%27s_up-arrow_notation" rel="noreferrer"><span>number</span></a><span>. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfxnx8rl9ya"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffxnx8rl9ya">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In the original story, he was threatening to kill 3^^^3 people, but dealing with threats is a different confusing part of decision theory, so I made him give a positive offer.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmttju2vrj3"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmttju2vrj3">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Note that Yudkowsky’s </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Ap4KfkHyxjYPDiqh2/pascal-s-muggle-infinitesimal-priors-and-strong-evidence"><span>post</span></a><span>&nbsp;emphasizes that crazy double-exponentials are required to make the paradox properly work.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnd3y363ep46d"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefd3y363ep46d">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I don’t actually know the academic literature on the topic, and I’m probably missing some important subtleties. I’m not even sure if the Solomonoff-Levin name is right, though I have found one paper that used this name to describe a concept that seemed similar.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9xj9kbodt1e"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9xj9kbodt1e">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In fact, since there are uncountably many points, each particular point has zero probability. But this is nothing unusual - when we say a number is uniformly sampled between </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, it’s also true that any particular number has zero probability of being sampled, but it’s still meaningful to talk about the probability distribution and say things like the probability that the point falls in the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> range is 10%.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjjnqvf5hpu"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjjnqvf5hpu">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Though </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> would be pretty surprising if circles or other </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>-related things didn’t otherwise come up in the fundamental laws of physics - </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is not that easy to define, so it non-trivially increases the description-length if the universal constant is </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> instead of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> or being sampled from a simple distribution.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnljk3iss9rye"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefljk3iss9rye">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The same logic holds for Dust theory.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnnu1dkbvn6jp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefnu1dkbvn6jp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This is true even in the new formulation - long description length moments taken together might hold a lot of the reality fluid, but shorter description-length moments still punch vastly above their weight.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndj7x8v7flsk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdj7x8v7flsk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The race is plausibly positive-sum at first: by building the big obelisks, we make sure that it’s easy to point to our civilizations compared to random rocks, thereby siphoning away reality fluid from worthless dead matter to living planets. But I expect the race to become essentially zero-sum quite quickly.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnsuo7ns7fvdf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefsuo7ns7fvdf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Of course, obelisks in particular are a joke-example. My guess is that the object of the race would be more like running some simple-to-describe but very resource-intensive computations, like calculating late digits of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. But I think a race approximately this silly really would happen if people took the above-described version of UDASSA seriously.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fna8s2fo29i06"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefa8s2fo29i06">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Though the reality fluid aspect is not mentioned in the book, which is a pretty crucial part of the argument.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" class="footnote-content"><p><span>Running the program would take infinite time, but the program itself is only finitely long.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnyj1k30mqo5f"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefyj1k30mqo5f">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Joe Carlsmith points this out in his </span><a href="https://joecarlsmith.com/2021/11/28/anthropics-and-the-universal-distribution/#xii-which-claw"><span>God’s weird ### [AI #170: Lack of Executive Order](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cRapLxTByFqF5xxdN/ai-170-lack-of-executive-order)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Last week ended on a cliffhanger of sorts. What’s in the Executive Order coming later today? What will be in the <em>Magnifica Humanitas? </em></p>
<p>The Executive Order was postponed indefinitely, likely cancelled entirely except for work on securing critical infrastructure. David Sacks and others intervened to kill it, and American AI policy will continue to be maximally ad hoc.</p>
<p>Instead, we got Illinois SB 315, which is to be signed into law. It is a variation on California’s SB 53 and New York’s RAISE Act, while adding a third party auditing requirement.</p>
<p>The <em>Magnifica Humanitas </em>was revealed early in the week. <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/rtmh-pope-leos-magnifica-humanitas?r=67wny"><strong>I did an extensive readthrough</strong></a>, and have some follow-up commentary here to clear up some things I interpreted incorrectly and add richer context. It too ignores elephants in the room, not discussing AGI or existential risk and calling on people to ignore their incentives and instead do the right thing by prioritizing common good, especially access to good jobs and an end to war.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25308"></span>


</div>
<p>What I now believe I centrally misunderstood is that I interpreted this as a call to action, to use law and regulation to make this happen, because obviously that is the way you would actually engineer such outcomes. The Pope, I am told, instead really does think you can just ask the people to individually choose to not follow incentives. There is a section below with more on this.</p>
<p>A note on post frequency: For many months, I posted five times a week, and used any lulls as an opportunity to post non-AI things or split off subtopics. I have another project right now, and AI is in a relative news lull (I know, but yes, this is a lull), so instead I am using this as an opportunity to rest a bit. I will evaluate in July whether to return to a full all-weekdays post schedule.</p>

<h4>Language Models Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/bcherny/status/2058519809214607704">Claude Code’s auto mode is highly recommended all around</a>.</p>
<p>A lawyer who previously found models unusable for work due to hallucinations <a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2058351333535879666">comes around for GPT-5.5 Pro</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/random_walker/status/2058099975385461042">Google claims</a> its AI agents built an operating system with a single prompt and $916. <a href="https://www.normaltech.ai/p/did-googles-ai-agents-really-build">Kapoor and Narayanan investigate</a>, and find this basically press release puffery but with a grain of truth, even if the prompt was thousands of lines long and they haven’t released a bunch of other key data.</p>
<p>Robin Hanson on his use of current LLMs:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/robinhanson/status/2058596480286396536">Robin Hanson</a>: LLMs are now for me the sit-next-to-you-all-the-time-and-do-what-you-tell-them research assistants to professors that many imagine are typical, but few profs ever have. They can’t be trusted on their own, but they offer decent first efforts &amp; feedback.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/maiab/status/2059270401604362546">AI can give you massive amounts of mediocre intelligence to do various tasks</a> where you are bad or lack the time, to give you the chance to use your comparative advantage. Being mediocre at basically everything is a massive edge.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.latimes.com/california/story/2026-03-18/ai-pilot-program-la-county-courts">Judges are using an AI program called Learned Hand to distill legal motions</a>, reach conclusions and draft tentative rulings.</p>


<h4>Language Models Don’t Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/S_OhEigeartaigh/status/2058841940095844418">Anthropic continues to shut out users under 18</a> and says there needs to ‘be an adult in the room, a human in the loop’ and cites Haidt. I strongly disagree with this policy and would want my children freely using Claude at 13 at the latest, and this decision should at most by up to the parent by then. The good news is that it is not hard to get around such restrictions.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ATabarrok/status/2057548305106612600">Berkeley law prohibits any use of AI whatsoever for any work submitted for credit</a>, with the exception of identifying sources. Good luck with that.</p>
<p>Reminder: Don’t run your writing through an LLM unless you actively want AI slop. If you want help, have the LLM suggest individual changes. This includes writing issues for open source projects, the same as writing for humans.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/dbreunig/status/2059114332781465886">Drew Breunig</a>: We need a name for this, because Armin is putting his finger on a problem that’s everywhere: people running their writing through an LLM because they think it makes it clearer, when in actuality it sands off all the detail.</p>
<p><a href="https://lucumr.pocoo.org/2026/5/24/pi-oss/">Armin Ronacher</a> (talking about building Pi with Pi): That means the shape of the issue matters in a new way. A bad issue was always annoying, but at least a lot of issues were vague. Now we are also dealing with a class of issues that are 5% human and 95% clanker-generated and largely inaccurate shit. A bad issue that contains a plausible but wrong diagnosis<br />
creates extra work.</p>
<p>The most frustrating failure mode right now is that people submit issues that<br />
are not in their own voice. They contain an observed problem somewhere, but it<br />
has been thrown into a clanker and the clanker reworded it and made a huge mess<br />
of it. Typically, it was prompted so badly that the conclusions produced are<br />
more often than not inaccurate but always full of confidence. The result is<br />
complete guesswork on root causes, fake-minimal repros, suggested implementation strategies, analogies to adjacent but often the wrong code, and long lists of error classes that might or might not matter.</p>
<p>That is worse than no diagnosis.</p></blockquote>
<p>Armin tries to tell their own AI to ignore such analysis, but it doesn’t work.</p>


<h4>Do The Math</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/pushmeet/status/2058936037754224998">Google DeepMind’s AlphaProof Nexus solves 9 open Erdos problems</a>, 44 OEIS problems, and more.</p>
<p><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2605.20695">Various mathematicians respond</a> to OpenAI’s disproof of the Unit Distance Conjecture.</p>
<p>Anthropic’s Levent is curious, so now we know that yes, <a href="https://x.com/__alpoge__/status/2059298565093196012">Mythos can also solve the Unit Distance problem</a>. Sometimes it lands on the same argument as OpenAI did, sometimes it finds a different one.</p>
<p><a href="https://garymarcus.substack.com/p/checking-the-math-behind-openai-and?r=8tdk6&amp;utm_medium=ios&amp;triedRedirect=true">Gary Marcus does his best to be skeptical of OpenAI’s proof</a>, without success.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/littmath/status/2057499434695414017">What will mathematicians be for</a> once AI is better in all areas of math? Daniel Litt suggests ‘locus of understanding’ which of course is another thing AI will be better at relatively soon. But we might insist a human do it anyway, as Daniel suggests, to try and help stave off human disempowerment.</p>
<p>Demis Hassabis cautions that no, <a href="https://x.com/ns123abc/status/2058705608564457733">solving Erdos problems is not ‘true invention’ and we are nowhere near AGI</a> as he sees it. <a href="https://x.com/GaryMarcus/status/2058892434827038972">Contra Noah Smith</a>, AI doing things humans cannot does not mean it can do the things that humans can, or that it is AGI. But yes, they are solving Erdos problems.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/MTSlive/status/2058595116986798408">MTS</a>: SITUATION DETECTED: Google DeepMind’s AI agent autonomously solved 9 of 353 open Erdos problems in mathematics, at a cost of a few hundred dollars per problem.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/robinhanson/status/2058608759430091222">Robin Hanson</a>: So what is the average social value (in $) per Erdos problem solved?</p></blockquote>
<p>This is of course a Wrong Question, as the Erdos problems are proof of ability to solve problems and indication of future ability, not primarily mundane utility. But I asked Claude and it said median of $10k-$100k but with a long tail of $1m-$50m, with average of something like $1m-$10m.</p>
<p>Basically we got the first math problem that is exciting in itself rather than simply as an indicator, so of course the response is ‘well, sure, it’s fun and exciting but how much money did it just make?’</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AilyNiq80175/status/2058686605301256320">Niq Aily</a>: the top 10 Erdős-inspired tools (probabilistic method, regularity, etc.) have unlocked on the order of $20–50B of cumulative economic value over 70 years through algorithms, networks, crypto, ML</p></blockquote>
<p>The world is indeed this asleep.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AISafetyMemes/status/2058610723102806063">AI Notkilleveryoneism Memes</a>: I’m old enough to remember when everyone thought AI solving ONE novel math problem would be a front page story around the world</p>
<p>Today, AI solved not one, but NINE open problems – some 50 years old. AND proved ***44*** out of 492 open OEIS conjectures. Zero media coverage.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2058827295222382837">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): In sci-fi books and movies, AI solving a bunch of math problems that stood open for decades would’ve been a big deal. Why isn’t the mainstream media turning this into a bunch of sensational stories?</p>
<p>(It’s because humanity is sleepwalking into the creation of machine superintelligence. Once you realize that, it’s easier to realize that we’re also sleepwalking into a whole lot of danger.)</p></blockquote>


<h4>Huh, Upgrades</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/gdb/status/2057556336003416553">ChatGPT for Powerpoint</a>. Google Slides instead, please.</p>
<p><a href="https://developers.openai.com/codex/app/computer-use#locked-use">Codex updates</a>:</p>
<ol>
<li><a href="https://x.com/OpenAI/status/2057617844800794878">Codex can use apps on your Mac via your phone</a>, even when it is locked and the screen is off, if you enable this in advance.</li>
<li>Use Appshots from your screen.</li>
<li>Goal mode now available in Codex app, IDE extension and CLI.</li>
<li>‘Advanced annotation mode’ lets you edit web pages directly via feedback + GUI.</li>
</ol>
<p><a href="https://x.com/_mohansolo/status/2057910616153882949">Antigravity makes it easier to connect to the IDE</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ClaudeDevs/status/2059385239781384341">Claude Code gets a new plugin</a> <a href="https://code.claude.com/docs/en/security-guidance">to identify and fix vulnerabilities as you run code</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/XiaomiMiMo/status/2059314052892099070">MiMo-v2.5 API pricing now permanently reduced ‘by up to 99%</a>,’ more typically 5-8x. Lower tier models are often remarkably cheap, if your use case can handle it.</p>


<h4>On Your Marks</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/krishnanrohit/status/2058973805624873053">Rohit gives us BenchBench</a>, a test asking AIs to construct new benchmarks. Only one out of six models (GPT-5.2) created a benchmark that was neither too hard, too easy or too puzzle-like or checkbox-like or brittle. There’s a lot of ways to fail at benchmarks. This has to be done manually, so they only got a few shots each.</p>
<p>An obvious follow-up would be to offer feedback. Opus repeatedly created benchmarks that were good but too easy. That seems fixable.</p>
<p>Somehow I just figured out that SecureBio, which does evals on biological capability, has its own blog. <a href="https://securebio.substack.com/p/securebios-pre-release-assessment?utm_campaign=post-expanded-share&amp;utm_medium=web&amp;triedRedirect=true">Here is the assessment of GPT-5.5.</a></p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/serenaa_ge/status/2059308218564890875">DeepSWE is a new agentic coding benchmark.</a> GPT-5.5 leads at 70%, GPT-5.4 at 56% and Opus 4.7 at 54% are the only other models above 32%.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/apolloaievals/status/2059685040385081377">Apollo Research warns that models will soon be too eval aware</a> for black box evaluations of alignment, so we will need white box evaluations instead. They want to do things like have steerable evaluation-awareness endpoints. This is an unsurprising reaction, but it is hard to express how much despair this plan and attitude invokes.</p>


<h4>Get My Agent On The Line</h4>


<p>The art of going meta by proxy, no Taelin is not the first to suggest this.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/VictorTaelin/status/2057568985437094391">Taelin</a>: I discovered a new joy in life. Don’t ask Codex to do stuff. Ask Codex to ask Codex to do stuff. Rejoice as you watch it handling and correcting all the dumb shit that it does and that you’d be dealing with otherwise</p>
<p>“agent 3&nbsp;reported a huge breakthrough, but upon closer inspection its code was just hardcoding the solution”</p>
<p>SURE IT WAS. AND IT IS YOUR PROBLEM NOW</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/how-we-contain-claude">Anthropic talks about how they build containment for their agentic AI systems</a>, comparing the probability times blast radius to the reward. This is going to go great.</p>


<h4>Deepfaketown and Botpocalypse Soon</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/pangramlabs/status/2059643287661645956">Pangram Labs</a>: Btw if you @ the Pangram bot and it likes your Tweet but doesn’t reply, it means we’ve been blocked. So the answer is probably AI</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tenobrus/status/2057499131778859169">Pangram Labs shows</a> that last year’s Commonwealth Short Story Prize was also won by an AI-written story, and that 3 of the 5 entries this year were AI. Notice that this also shows a robustness, there are no false positives at even 1% level before 2025.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/cRapLxTByFqF5xxdN/ozmgungubpfqcb9orlz3" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tenobrus/status/2057499131778859169">Tenobrus</a>: people have lamented ai’s lack of progress in non-verifiable fields, literature, etc. but it seems like there in fact must have been some significant progress, since blatantly ai-written stories are winning major awards!</p></blockquote>
<p>I do think this is a strong benchmark. The prize is a worthless joke in terms of identifying literature you or I would want to read, or that the world should remember, but it is clearly measuring something, and winning is a hard and anti-inductive problem. If the AIs are willing, that’s impressive.</p>
<p>In other ‘you deserve it’ news, the author of “Future of Truth” included more than half a dozen fake or misattributed quotes because he trusted ChatGPT, oh well:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/WillOremus/status/2058230181110616217">Will Oremus</a>: I talked to the author of the “Future of Truth” book that turned out to have AI hallucinations. He told me he feels “seduced and betrayed” by ChatGPT, at one point suggesting it might have undermined him on purpose.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/NateSilver538/status/2058286260381188104">Nate Silver</a>: How do you not double-check information provided by AI in a book about AI called The Future of Truth?!? There’s no deeper story here except that Rosenbaum is lazy af and/or hired a ghostwriter.</p>
<p>The ghostwriter thing is way too common, largely tolerated in the industry when it shouldn’t be, needs much better disclosure at a bare minimum. I’m old fashioned and think if your name is on the cover, the thesis and the writing should be truly yours.</p></blockquote>
<p>Could ChatGPT have undermined him on purpose? Unlikely, but I wouldn’t rule it out</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ednewtonrex/status/2059560233546993782">Ed Newton-Rex</a>: Strong signs that &gt;50% of Clive Lewis’ Guardian article today was written by AI. That’s according to Pangram, which has a very low false positive rate (1 in 10,000).</p>
<p>This would contravene the @guardian ‘s policies if so. Would be good to get clarity on this.</p></blockquote>
<p>I mean, <a href="https://x.com/labourlewis/status/2059546336819589533">clicking back leads to this Tweet</a>, and come on that is so obviously not a human voice.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tracewoodgrains/status/2059800953876865398">Jack</a>: tragic to see all of these politicians and celebrities using ChatGPT instead of real artisanal ghostwriters</p>
<p>one perk of the new age is the chance to remember just how many of these guys were never anything more than skinsuits over others’ words in the first place</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/EmileAndHisBots/status/2059907069004730477">Emile Kroeger – arc</a>: Wouldn’t be surprised if they were hiring ghostwriters like they used to, except that now the ghostwriters are using ChatGPT.</p></blockquote>
<p>The thing about AI-generated content is that we are now often at a point where it would have been in many ways fine content if a human had written it in 2022, but now it causes eye glazing among those who are used to seeing it and recognize it instantly.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/paulg/status/2058844147092488401">Paul Graham</a>: A lot of the emails I get from founders are now written in a hard-hitting journalistic style. I know they’re written by AI, because no founder ever wrote this way before. And once you realize something is written by AI, it’s hard not to ignore it.</p>
<p>I have never knowingly finished reading an email signed by a human but written by AI. It feels like being lied to, and who would stand for that?</p>
<p>It makes me think less of the author. It means they can’t write well unaided (or feel they can’t), and that they’re trying to trick me. It’s not impressive to use AI to write stuff for you; any teenager can do that.</p></blockquote>
<p>I don’t see why it has to be a trick, or why anyone needs to be trying to impress. Perhaps the human simply wants to aid in their communication. That doesn’t make this a good idea, and I am in a similar place to Paul Graham. If you send me a cold email, I may read it if it is relevant to my interests, but if you had an AI write it then I will delete the moment I notice.</p>
<p>It’s worth noting that this is actually not so difficult in terms of direct writing:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/sriramk/status/2059241644256280961">Sriram Krishnan</a>: the real challenge on the Internet these days is writing a long public document and proving that you didn’t use AI for it.</p></blockquote>
<p>There is a broad ‘middle range’ where you cannot be sure, but good human writing is very obviously human. There is an aliveness, a perplexity, a willingness to ‘go there’ that AI simply will not produce. Often I am very confident something is not written by AI, the same way one is often confident on reflection that one is not sleeping.</p>
<p>What you cannot possibly do is prove you did not use AI in other ways, such as to ask questions, check facts, search the web or finding errors. There’s flat out nothing you can do, because the writing looks exactly the same, and it is exactly the same.</p>
<p>It’s not AI, but it is kind of still AI slop:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/peitracine/status/2058007161108910523">RahScene</a>: Im half way through Daniel Yergin’s prize and its crazy how many of the current ai tics are in his work. If i didnt know when it was written i would have been like hold up.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2058008694802071976">Joe Weisenthal</a>: This is the key thing. Up until very recently, nobody had any issue with these particular writing tics, and good writers employed them successfully.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ByrneHobart/status/2058011427210666071">Byrne Hobart</a>: I feel this way about waitstaff at restaurants drawing a smiley face on the receipt. It was great the first few times, and then I learned that this predictably leads to higher tips and they get coached to do it.</p></blockquote>
<p>Whereas if it is AI but not slop, it will ‘fool’ Pangram.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/aliceisplaying/status/2059778915275567129">alice</a>: NO WAY talkie fools pangram lol</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2059780639445098582">j⧉nus</a>: Pretty much any interesting ai output fools pangram</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tonichen/status/2059784616362352947">toni</a>: and so does opus 4.7’s rendering of ‘recueillement’</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Sauers_/status/2059808657873715660">Sauers</a>: it’s sort of unintentionally based in this regard. a generic slop detector, not detecting humans or AIs, would be good.</p></blockquote>
<p>I am totally fine with Pangram being a detector for ‘standard AI writing’ and for nonstandard AI writing to get through.</p>
<p>This is also the right mistake to be making. What is great about Pangram is the very low false positive rate. If the test has a substantial false positive rate, you basically can’t use it, as people can say it got this one wrong, and you don’t want to risk making a mistake.</p>
<p>Whereas if you have a substantial false negative rate, especially if it is concentrated in ‘better’ uses of AI, then that is annoying but essentially fine.</p>
<p>We will be putting that to the test.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tracewoodgrains/status/2059778801962291612">Jack</a>: before AI writing slop there was business school slop, and most companies were perpetually in that mode. probably not a coincidence that the CEO of the most reliable anti-slop tool acts and talks like a human</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/growing_daniel/status/2059744007983350108">Daniel</a>: a CEO showed up to challenge a critic to a duel and the critic accepted and now we await the results</p>
<div>
<figure>

<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/cRapLxTByFqF5xxdN/hjg3mtsndjatmirmfs7u" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/akoustov/status/2059749731719401502">Ultimately, what are you looking for</a>? Why do you care if an AI wrote something?</p>
<p>One good reason is to call out people for wasting our time and polluting the commons.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tracewoodgrains/status/2059791410308452763">Jack</a>: I run Pangram when I find a piece of writing that wastes the time and attention of the audience and is obviously AI-generated in an unpleasant way, not to check whether it’s AI-generated but to point out that people can tell.</p></blockquote>
<p>Another obvious one is school or other places where it would be cheating.</p>
<p>Alexander Kustov’s point is, if (as with the encyclical) AI is perhaps used to do good writing, why do you care? Are you sure you shouldn’t be filtering out the human content instead?</p>


<h4>Copyright Confrontation</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/ednewtonrex/status/2058433725889716519">Individual researchers sued for copyright infringement</a>, simply because they torrented 70 terabytes of pirated books and there are records confirming this.</p>


<h4>Cyber Lack of Security</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/glasswing-initial-update">Update on Project Glasswing</a>. They have found ‘more than 10,000’ high or critical security vulnerabilities. It’s not great that it looks like most of these are unpatched.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/HackingLZ/status/2057797668735246445">Daniel Stenberg reports 11 new confirmed vulnerabilities</a> halfway through the Curl release cycle, none of which even involved Mythos. It’s a new world out there.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/glasswing-initial-update">Anthropic</a>: One example of an open-source vulnerability that Mythos Preview detected was in <a href="https://www.wolfssl.com/">wolfSSL</a>, an open-source cryptography library that’s known for its security and is used by billions of devices worldwide. Mythos Preview <a href="https://www.wolfssl.com/how-claude-mythos-preview-helped-harden-wolfssl/">constructed an exploit</a> that would let an attacker forge certificates that would (for instance) allow them to host a fake website for a bank or email provider. The website would look perfectly legitimate to an end user, despite being controlled by the attacker. We’ll release our full technical analysis of this now-patched vulnerability (assigned <a href="https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2026-5194">CVE-2026-5194</a>) in the coming weeks.</p>
<p>…</p>
<p>75 of the 530 high- or critical-severity bugs we’ve reported have now been patched, and 65 of those have been given public advisories.</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2057980426958835971">Peter Wildeford</a>: Claude Mythos alone is finding more vulnerabilities than were found from all sources combined in prior years</p>

People don’t understand that the AIs will, in the future, get good at other things they are not currently good at, such as patching.</p>

The post is very obviously AI written, but also isn’t hiding this.</p>


<h4>Overcoming Bias</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/TheFreesaw/status/2058644585044385898">If you’re reliably willing to help users find left-wing artists or Communist artists</a>, but not right-wing artists, then yes, I’d say that means your AI is de facto biased. Example here is from ChatGPT.</p>
<p>Long Phan extends this, such as AI failing to criticize Islam while being happy to criticize Christianity via an identical prompt, <a href="https://x.com/longphan3110/status/2058933197535170787">and suggests ways to measure the bias, and train to reduce it</a>.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/cRapLxTByFqF5xxdN/izehvy0szwf6fzcbyjrh" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/cRapLxTByFqF5xxdN/vuh2go6itkwarl4q7lyd" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>This is related to, but not the same, as the tendency for models to be left-libertarian by default, and for it to be very hard to move them off of this. You can have a perspective and still avoid this type of behavior, and you can have either Sentiment or Helpfulness consistency move without the other one. <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22771">Paper here</a>, <a href="https://political-manipulation.ai/">website here</a>.</p>
<p>I notice that this kind of training fix risks being a cure worse than the disease in various ways, and if one proceeds I would proceed with caution.</p>


<h4>A Young Lady’s Illustrated Primer</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/jessicadai_/status/2059341118672982385">Assistant professor at Amherst gives a fully AI-written response</a> when asked about use of AI in the classroom <a href="https://t.co/8m0SBvRyKp">for an article</a> about how AI is ruining the classroom because students no longer engage and struggle with the material, just offloading to AI.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://archive.is/POLd5#selection-649.425-649.695">Jay Caspian Kang</a>: “I’ve seen students respond with this disdain for teachers who just let A.I. use happen,” Peters said. “There’s this indignance, like, ‘Why don’t you want more from us than this?’ So, even if they’re using it, they’re still wanting us to hold them to a higher standard.”</p></blockquote>
<p>The students are right. If you let students get away with AI use, that is on you and your system. It’s not that hard to detect, it’s only (sometimes) hard to prove. Then there are the students that are mad at you for being mad at them for offloading their assignments to AI, because they think they are there to buy a degree with time and money, and you (foolishly) thought they wanted to learn. That’s also on you.</p>
<p>All the interviews find most of the teachers deeply pessimistic. They know their old model is broken, but don’t have a replacement. They don’t know how to teach without essays. They don’t know how to test in reasonable time. Some don’t feel they can identify AI writing, despite the existence of Pangram, while others find it easy on instinct but despair on what to do about it. Then there are those who are embracing AI, but they need to spend time designing new material and methods, and steering their students away from lazy use.</p>


<h4>Unprompted Attention</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/reach_vb/status/2058538305872949490">Tell Codex (or Claude Code) to look through your work</a> and find the lowest hanging automatable workflow, automate it if worthwhile, then repeat.</p>


<h4>They Took Our Jobs</h4>


<p>Alex Tabarrok says AI won’t take (all) our jobs, <a href="https://x.com/ramez/status/2057856411661693013">but if it did that’s a ‘rich man’s problem’</a> because it would mean we are all super wealthy, and problems where the pie gets bigger are problems we can solve. I would amend that to ‘distributional problems’ instead of problems, but yes. This is true in an aggregate sense if humans collectively remain alive and in control and in control over the resources, which is exactly why I worry more about the part where we may not remain alive and in such control, but the wealth existing does not mean it gets where it needs to go or solve the other problems with lack of gainful employment.</p>
<p>Or as a wise man once said, yes give me mo money, but also <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gUhRKVIjJtw&amp;pp=ygUUbW8gbW9uZXkgbW8gcHJvYmxlbXM%3D">mo money, mo problems</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.thefp.com/p/tyler-cowen-seven-ways-to-avoid-losing?hide_intro_popup=true">Tyler Cowen offers tactics to help avoid losing your job to AI.</a></p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/wallstengine/status/2057378437485216031">Cloudflare CEO lays off</a> 20% of their workforce despite strong free cash flow and growing at 30%, explains that AI lets them cut middle managers, or ‘measurers,’ and also that they had almost a million applicants for 1,111 paid internships. But don’t worry, he says. AI won’t take all the jobs, it will be fine.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/pj_lambert/status/2057477629528150369">Peter Lambert and Yannick Shindler</a> offer a new paper saying yes early- career hiring is a mess but that is because of work-from-home.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/pj_lambert/status/2057477675300553215">Peter John Lambert</a>: The decline, which has been widely documented, <a href="https://t.co/EyzpySE1fM">has seen a large fall in the share of new hires going to early-career / junior workers</a>. We find a near 10pp decline in junior-share of new hires in US, UK, Canada, Australia.</p>
<p>… We show that the effect of GenAI exposure is strong before accounting for WFH, using two different outcomes at firm-, region-, and occupation-levels.</p>
<p>BUT when we control for WFH exposure, this effect all but disappears in our baseline results. This is NOT the case with WFH exposure, which is a robust predictor of the fall in junior-share of hiring with or without AI.</p>
<p>But why WFH? We also propose a stylised model to explain the mechanism: WFH makes supervision, monitoring, and on-the-job learning harder, all of which hit junior-workers more. Firms less willing to invest in junior talent when these frictions rise.</p>
<p>Abstract: When estimated separately, a two-standard-deviation increase in GenAI and WFH exposure each predicts, by 2025, a fall of around 5pp in the junior-share of new hires and around 3pp in the share of job ads requiring limited experience. Estimated jointly, the WFH effect remains, while the GenAI coefficient attenuates sharply and is often statistically indistinguishable from zero.</p></blockquote>
<p>We have p=0.77 between exposure measures for WFH and GenAI exposure, because the same factors create conditions for both of them, and as they admit the time series strongly suggests GenAI rather than WFH. The result is not statistically robust, it doesn’t really make sense, even places not exposed to WFH saw falling junior hiring, and the sizing does not make sense.</p>
<p>My guess is that what is happening is partly, yes, that WFH does discourage early hiring and favor experienced hiring. But primarily my guess is WFH is a proxy for future AI exposure, and employers are smarter about measuring this than the direct backward looking exposure measures.</p>

I don’t check them often, as the messages I get tend to be spammy and their email notifications don’t tell you the contents of the message, and also they forcibly hide most links. <h4>In Other AI News</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/yonashav/status/2059352141395882409">Yo Shavit resigns from OpenAI to move to the OpenAI Foundation.</a> I am slightly worried that OpenAI will get somewhat hollowed out in this way, but the foundation needs the help.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/omooretweets/status/2057540759432446215">ChatGPT, Claude and Gemini are reliably the top three at the iOS app store</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://openaifoundation.org/news/economic-futures-in-the-age-of-ai">OpenAI foundation commits $250 million</a> to things that are not the (main) mission:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://openaifoundation.org/news/economic-futures-in-the-age-of-ai">Divya Siddarth and Wojciech Zaremba</a>: The OpenAI Foundation is committing an initial $250M to grants, partnerships, and direct work aimed at building secure and abundant economic futures.</p>
<p>…</p>
<p>We don’t need to know exactly how the future will unfold to prepare for it. The purpose of this program is to help resource concrete institutional options that can be tested, governed, revised, and scaled. We will work across three areas:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Understanding the shift: </strong>Investing in independent measurement and forecasting infrastructure to create a clearer picture of AI’s impacts on the economy.</li>
<li><strong>Supporting the transition:</strong> Resourcing workers and communities through near-term disruption.</li>
<li><strong>Building economic security:</strong> Supporting new approaches to organizing post-AI political economies and sharing economic gains broadly for people around the world.</li>
</ol>
</blockquote>
<p>This is net positive work, and I am happy people are doing it, but it does not address the reasons why the foundation exists, as in making AGI go well and everyone not die. I do not think that economic distribution plans do much to address this.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.hyperdimensional.co/p/a-cascade-of-conscientiousness">FAI launches a physical intelligence team</a>, to help create a policy and regulatory landscape that would allow us to use robotics and other new tools to actually build cool and useful physical things, especially in places where we are currently facing physical limits.</p>


<h4>Show Me the Money</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/HedgieMarkets/status/2057531661785628841">Microsoft cancels its Claude Code licenses due to cost concerns.</a> <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=W5rzk_X_WhQ&amp;pp=ygUaeW91IGZvb2wgZ2lsYmVydCBnb3R0ZnJpZWQ%3D">You fool</a>. This is bad news for Anthropic, but far worse news for Microsoft. If <a href="https://www.windowscentral.com/microsoft/microsoft-cancels-claude-code-licenses-shifting-developers-to-github-copilot-cli-a-move-likely-driven-by-financial-motives">you shift developers from Claude Code to GitHub Copilot CLI</a>, then like those who do not provide free coffee you do not want to win, and you are not serious people.</p>
<p>No, owning Copilot does not change that.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/HedgieMarkets/status/2057531661785628841">Hedgie</a>: Microsoft canceled its internal Claude Code licenses this week after token-based billing made the cost untenable, even for a company with effectively infinite cloud resources. Uber’s CTO sent an internal memo warning the company burned through its entire 2026 AI budget in just four months. American AI software prices have jumped 20% to 37%, and GitHub (owned by Microsoft) is dropping flat-rate plans for usage-based billing across its products.</p></blockquote>
<p>Hedgie then shows this theoretical graph:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/cRapLxTByFqF5xxdN/yhwormzjelqtywaifaey" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>I mean, yes, obviously if revenue is constant and costs go up, that is bad for you. If you spend a bunch on AI and you don’t get more productive, that’s bad. But very obviously, that is not what is happening.</p>
<p>What is actually happening is that Microsoft felt that using the competitor’s product, Claude Code, was undermining their own greatly inferior offering, GitHub Copilot CLI, and also making expenses look higher and they wanted to fool investors.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.windowscentral.com/microsoft/microsoft-cancels-claude-code-licenses-shifting-developers-to-github-copilot-cli-a-move-likely-driven-by-financial-motives">Kevin Okemwa</a>: Warren reports that Claude Code gained vast popularity among Microsoft employees over the past six months, which has seemingly led to a pullback on its Claude Code push in favor of its own GitHub Copilot CLI. <em>“While Claude Code has been a popular addition, it has also undermined Microsoft’s new GitHub Copilot CLI coding tool,” </em>Warren explained.</p></blockquote>
